* Each theme has **four colour variants** and **four style variants**: the colour variants are the accents in their own order, one place on, one hue in seven tones (`monotone`: every variant colour is the first) and three places on, the seventh colour of each being the theme's second dark colour; the style variants are
  Original (a pale fill with the colour's outline, a dark fill with white text, a plain colour, white with a heavy outline), Gradient (soft gradients and a soft shadow), Outline (white or pale, thick outlines, no shadow) and Raised (the first variant's fills with a deeper shadow). **The variants pick lines 1 to 3 only** (`kit.rs`): a connector's list holds three
  entries and an entry past a list reads the no-theme table. The theme's `Look` gives each its character: Tidepool and Plumline round corners (`vt:lineEx` `rndg`) and use wide soft shadows, Cinderglow heavy lines and hard shadows, Inkstone fine lines and no shadow, Fernwood thin lines.
* Fonts are open families (Liberation Sans and Serif, DejaVu Sans and Serif): a face is named in a drawing and never embedded (the check list, "No font embedding"), and none is a face the project may not ship. `THEMEVAL` answers `Font` with the theme's body face (`a:minorFont`); the heading face is in the part and is read by nothing.
* A theme part is written as the samples' are: `a:theme` with `a:themeElements` (`a:clrScheme`, `a:fontScheme`, `a:fmtScheme`), `a:objectDefaults` and `a:extraClrSchemeLst`, every extension of R7 under its URI with the `vt:` namespace declared by the child, a declaration with `standalone="yes"`, six entries in each list of the effect scheme and three in
  the connector scheme's, one more `vt:lineStyle` than lines, four variants of each kind. A part written reads back as the definition it was made from (`tests/it/theme_write.rs`, a generated test over any definition), and no colour, modifier or list the reader would cut is written.

**What it costs** (`tests/it/theme_cost.rs`, `theme_file.rs`: counted, not timed, as `diacraft_calc::work` and `diacraft_vsdx::work` count; the page of 500 shapes and 300 connectors). The first `theme.apply` on the 800 shapes (2,400 links) is **one commit of 204,347 steps, the steps of the recalculation of the whole page as it was**: a theme works the page out once and no more;
the same theme again works out nothing; another theme, `theme.variant` and "no theme" are one commit each. A page of 10,000 shapes that each hold five scheme cells and a lock is one commit of 520,000 steps, and the locks are looked up through **one resolver for the page** (a unit test counts the resolvers made). A drawing with 255 themes costs an apply and a variant two commits and the list none; a hostile part of 200 sixteen-modifier fills, a variant and a removal 2 commits of 91 steps. The writer pairs themes by hashing: each theme's bytes at most twice, whatever the parts share (counted).
What the fixture lacks: it holds no groups, no master whose shapes hold theme formulas and no number cached under one, so the recalculation writes nothing; a foreign page writes what its theme cells' readers now give. A page of more than `MAX_THEMED_SHAPES` (200,000) shapes to change is refused whole.

**For the window (the Design tab's Themes and Variants galleries).** Themes: one item for each theme of `theme.list` (its `colours` give the thumbnail: the accents on the dark and light colours, the `fonts` the sample text), preceded by "No Theme" (`theme.apply {theme: "none"}`); the current one is `page.theme`, which the gallery marks (`custom` for a theme the file brought, which no item
marks). The gallery's menu chooses the page or every page: `scope` `page` or `all`. Variants: the four of the current theme, `variants[i].colours` the thumbnail and `name` the tooltip, sending `theme.variant {variant: i}`; the current one is `page.colors` (with `page.styles`: `colors` and `styles` can differ after a `colors` or `styles` call, and then no variant is marked). A theme applied is shown at once, the renderer asking the theme each time it draws.
Not here, and not hidden by a control that would send them (Quick Styles are `shape.quick_style`): swapping the colours, effects, connectors or fonts of a theme for another's (P1 and P2), "Remove Theme" from chosen shapes (P1), a paste or a duplicate (it keeps the links its source had) and an option to turn "Apply theme to new shapes" off (P1), "Stop a theme reaching a shape" as a control (the locks are read, not set), the theme's background colour (P2), and custom themes (P2).

## Transparency, patterns, gradients, shadows and Quick Styles

The fill's and line's transparency, the pattern fill, the cap and compound type of a line, the gradient of a fill or a line, the shadow, and the Quick Styles of the Home tab's
Shape Styles group. The renderer drew all of these from the cells before; these commands
and the Format Shape pane write them. Code: `crates/engine/src/formatting/` (`shape_cells.rs`, `effects.rs`, `quick.rs`), `cmd/effects.rs`, and in the window `format_fx.rs`, `panes/format_fx.rs`
and `ribbon/quick_styles.rs`. The reference app was not inspected and our output has not been opened in it by the project: every rule below is **ours** where it says so, and is listed in the report of the
task for the check list.

| Command | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `shape.format` (more parameters) | `fill_transparency`, `line_transparency` (percent, 0 to 100), `fill_pattern` (1 solid, 2 to 24 hatches and stipples), `fill_background` (`#RRGGBB`), `line_cap` (`round`, `flat`, `square`), `compound` (`single`, `double`, `thick_thin`, `thin_thick`, `triple`) | `{page, formatted}`, as before |
| `shape.gradient` | `ids`, `page`?, `target`? (`fill`, the default, or `line`), `on`?, `kind`? (`linear`, `radial`, `rectangular`, `path`) with `origin`?, or `direction`? (0 to 13), `angle`? (degrees), `rotate_with_shape`?, `stops`? (2 to 10 of `{position, color, transparency?}`) | `{page, target, formatted}` |
| `shape.shadow` | `ids`, `page`?, `on`?, `preset`? (`none`, `bottom_right`, `bottom`, `bottom_left`, `right`, `center`, `left`, `top_right`, `top`, `top_left`), `color`?, `transparency`?, `size`?, `blur`?, `angle`?, `distance`? | `{page, formatted}` |
| `shape.quick_style` | `ids`, `page`?, `row` (1 to 6) with `column` (1 to 7), or `variant` (0 to 3) | `{page, formatted, matrix, colour}` |
| `shape.quick_styles` (query) | `ids`, `page`? | `{page, current: {matrix, colour}, variants: [tile], styles: [tile]}`: the gallery for the first shape, each `tile` `{variant | row and column, matrix, colour, fill, line, weight, fill_transparency, gradient, shadow}` as the shape looks in that style |

Each is one undo step, and a call that would change nothing is none and does not make the drawing unsaved. `shape.formatting` also answers, for each shape, `fill_pattern`, `fill_transparency`,
`fill_background` (`null` unless the fill is a hatch or stipple), `line_transparency`, `line_cap`, `compound`, `gradient` and `line_gradient` (`null` or `{direction, kind, origin, angle, rotate_with_shape,
stops: [{position, color, transparency}]}`) and `shadow` (`null` or `{color, transparency, size, blur, angle, distance, kind}`).

* A transparency is a share on the half-percent steps files hold (a writer rounds to the nearest 0.005): 35.3 percent is 0.355. It writes `FillForegndTrans` and `LineColorTrans`; beside a
  hatch or stipple also `FillBkgndTrans` (the ground has its own); and with a gradient on, **the `GradientStopColorTrans` of every stop** of the section the gradient is in ("for one transparency over the whole shape,
  set the same number on every stop"), which is what the pane's slider does when it shows a transparency for a gradient's stop. A refusal names the key and "from 0 to 100 percent".
* Our rule. `fill_pattern` writes `FillPattern` and, where the shape had a gradient on, turns it off (`FillGradientEnabled` 0, its rows stay), because a pattern does not paint under a gradient.
  The gradients 25 to 40 of the old way are `shape.gradient`'s, and refused here in words. `fill_background` is the colour between the marks (`FillBkgnd`); the colour of the marks is `fill`.
* `line_cap` is what files draw: `round` 0, `flat` 1 (the number MS-VSDX names square draws a flat end) and `square` 2 (the number it names extended draws the projecting square).
* Our rule. A gradient turned on makes the shape paint a fill (or a line) at all: `FillPattern` 0 becomes 1, and `LinePattern` 0 becomes 1, because a pattern 0 paints nothing whatever else is set.
  Our rule: turned on with no stops it keeps the ones the shape has, or, when it has none, is made of two, the colour the shape has now and a lighter one (white, or a grey for a colour that is nearly white).
  Any gradient setting turns it on unless `on` is false, which writes 0 where it was on and leaves the rows. A shape the gradient is not on is left as it is by `on: false`.
* The stops are written **in position order from `IX` 0** as `GradientStopColor`, `GradientStopColorTrans` and `GradientStopPosition`; a row of the style or the master that the new list does not
  reach is **deleted here** (`Del`: a shape that follows the `Theme` style, which holds ten rows, keeps no stop it was not given), and a row the shape had deleted is brought back when a list needs it. Two to ten stops:
  a refusal says "from 2 to 10 stops". The layout is `kind` with `origin`, which `FillGradientDir`'s table gives, or `direction` itself; `angle` is degrees clockwise from the right as seen on the page, written
  in radians; `rotate_with_shape` is a fill's `RotateGradientWithShape`. `target: line` is the same for `LineGradientEnabled`, `LineGradientDir`, `LineGradientAngle` and the rows of `LineGradient`.
* The controls read a gradient section (the shape's, its master's and its styles' rows together) **only when it has at most 256 rows**, counted before any row is merged (`Resolver::effective_rows_within`: a file may hold a hundred thousand rows of stops, counts ten), and read a longer one as a section with no stops, so a gradient fill of such a shape shows the two fill colours; the picture draws its first ten rows. A command that writes a gradient writes the stops it is given from `IX` 0 and deletes at most the rows 3 to 9 the shape or its style holds. A Quick Style on 4,096 shapes that follow the `Theme` style and a gradient and a shadow on 4,096 shapes are each one pass and **one commit** (`formatfx_cost.rs`: counted, not timed), and one that would change nothing commits nothing.
* Our rule (leaves the sense open). The pane's Distance (points) and Angle (degrees) are the shadow's offset in polar form: the angle is **clockwise from the right as seen
  on the page**, so 90 is straight down. A theme's own `ShadowDirection` is DrawingML's bearing, clockwise from the right, and `ShapeShdwOffsetX` agrees with the distance times its cosine and `ShapeShdwOffsetY` with
  the negated distance times its sine, so what a theme gives and what the pane shows agree. The offset is paper inches along the page's axes with y up; the blur is paper inches.
* Our rule. Any shadow setting turns the shadow on, unless `on` is false (`ShdwPattern` 0; the other cells stay). A shadow that is off is made whole
  from the starting one (ours: black, 60 percent clear, the size of the shape, blurred 4 points, 4 points away at 45 degrees) with what is given; one that is on has **only what is given written**, so a
  shadow that follows its theme keeps following it for the rest. A shadow of the page's default (`ShapeShdwType` 0) is given the shape's own type 1 with its offsets and size as it shows them. (ours;   the galleries' contents are `named` only):
  the ten presets are `none` and the starting shadow in each of the eight directions and `center` (no distance, blurred 8 points).
* Our rule. A Quick Style is **two numbers**: an entry of the theme's lists, `QuickStyleLineMatrix`, `QuickStyleFillMatrix` and `QuickStyleEffectsMatrix`, and a colour,
  `QuickStyleLineColor`, `QuickStyleFillColor` and `QuickStyleShadowColor`. The gallery is the reference's two grids: four **Variant Styles**, entry 100 to 103 (the first to fourth of the page's style variant) in colour 100, and
  the **Theme Styles**, six rows of seven, row `r` column `c` being entry `r` in colour `99 + c` (the seven colours of the page's colour variant). The font cells are not written.
* Our rule. A shape without members, inside the shapes named, follows the page's `Theme` style for a family that has no style of its own or the root style, as `theme.apply` makes it; the six cells
  are written; and every cell of the line, fill and shadow families (and the ten stop rows of the fill gradient, and the scheme cells that send a shape to its page, 65534) **that does not read the theme's marker is written as
  the call `THEMEVAL()` with the marker as its value**, as the shapes of files the reference application saved hold them: **choosing a style replaces the colour a person set**, and a shape with a style of its own takes its fill
  from the theme. A shape that follows the `Theme` style has nothing but the six cells written. A page with no theme, or a drawing with no `Theme` style, is a failure in words ("apply a theme first (Design, Themes)").
  At most 4,096 shapes (the shapes inside the groups named counted): past it the command is refused in words and changes nothing. A connector's list holds three entries, so a row past 3 reads the no-theme table.
* `shape.quick_styles` gives each tile what the first shape selected **looks like in a copy of the document** with that style put on it (the same planning as `shape.quick_style`, no recalculation: a cell
  that holds the marker is asked of the theme as it is read), so a tile shows what a press makes, and the drawing is not changed (a test compares the session before and after). It is 46 copies of a document, which is a handful of
  pointers each, and 46 readings of one shape.
* Our rule. **What is not painted is not shown** by `shape.formatting`: a shape that paints no fill reads no fill transparency, ground or gradient, one that draws no line no line transparency, cap, compound type or
  gradient, and one that paints neither no shadow. So the look of a text box does not change in a theme variant for what the picture does not draw. A fill of the old way, `FillPattern` 25 to 40, reads as the gradient it
  stands for.
* **Files.** The cells ride in VSDX and `.diacraft` as they are. In an `.odg` the opacity of a fill and a line is `draw:opacity` and `svg:stroke-opacity`, a solid fill and a stroke are native, and **what the ODF styles do not hold
  natively here is carried in DiaCraft's namespaced extension and named in the save note** (`tests/it/formatfx_files.rs` saves each of the effects in the three formats and reads them back as they were).
* (with Q3 answered by measure). **An `.odg` says a gradient, a hatch, a stipple and a shadow in ODF's own markup**, for a new drawing and a new shape of a file (the
  model writer and the splice) and for a gradient or a shadow set on a shape of a file, and the cells ride in DiaCraft's data beside it, so the drawing reads back as it was (`crates/engine/tests/it/odg_fx.rs`: each of the 14 layouts,
  the 24 patterns and three shadows, from a new drawing and into a file; `crates/odg/tests/it/fx_native.rs`, and a property test over random looks). **A gradient** is a `draw:gradient` of DiaCraft's own in `office:styles`,
  named by its layout, its angle and its stops (`DiaCraft_20_gradient_20_...`), so the element is made from the name: `draw:angle` is 90 degrees less the cell's angle (LibreOffice 26.8.2.2 turns it counterclockwise from a
  start at the top, and reads a number with no unit as degrees), the colours of a radial or rectangular gradient run from the outer edge in (the last stop is `draw:start-color`), the stops beyond two or not at 0 and 1 are
  `loext:gradient-stop` children (the one form LibreOffice reads: it reads no `svg:linearGradient`), the path layout (13) is written as the rectangular one from the centre, and a layout from a corner or the middle of an edge has
  its stops at twice or 1.58 times their places, since LibreOffice ends such a gradient at half the distance to the far corner. **A hatch** (patterns 2 to 7, 13 to 16 and 19 to 23) is a `draw:hatch` named by the pattern and the
  colour of its marks, over the ground of `draw:fill-color` (`draw:rotation` and `draw:distance` follow the lines the renderer draws; LibreOffice draws every hatch line hairline thin, so the heavy bars of 13 to 16 are lighter
  there); **a stipple** (8 to 12, 17, 18 and 24), which the renderer draws as one flat mixture of its two colours, is that colour. **A shadow** is `draw:shadow`, its colour, `draw:shadow-opacity` (the complement of the share),
  its offsets (y runs down in the file and up in the cell) and `loext:shadow-blur` of four thirds of the cell's blur (DiaCraft's deviation is the blur over 1.5 and LibreOffice's about half its blur). **What ODF has no form
  for stays in the data:** a stop's transparency, a shadow's size and any type but 1, `RotateGradientWithShape`, a line's gradient, the exact angle, a cap and a compound line. **Another program's gradients, hatches and shadows
  stay as they are.** A hatch or a stipple set on a shape of a file is not written as a display form (the shape's fill stays the marks' colour) until the file is saved from the model.
* **For the window.** The Format Shape pane's Fill has **No fill, Solid fill, Gradient fill and Pattern fill**, and under the kind the shapes have: the colour and a transparency (a number box in percent over a slider); for a
  gradient the layout, the angle or the place it starts from, a bar with a handle for each stop, Add stop, Remove stop, and the colour, position and transparency of the stop selected, and Rotate with shape; for a pattern the grid of
  the 23 patterns (our own artwork: lines, grids and dots), the colour of the marks, the colour between them and the transparency. **Line** has No line, Solid line and Gradient line, the colour, transparency, width, compound type, dash
  type, cap type, rounding and ends. **Effects** has the Shadow group: Presets, Color, Transparency, Size, Blur, Angle and Distance, quiet while the shapes have no shadow; Reflection, Glow, Soft Edges, Bevel and 3-D Rotation stay
  quiet. The window's commands are `format.gradient` (also the edits of one stop: `stop`, `stop_color`, `line_stop_color`, `stop_position`, `stop_transparency`, `add_stop`, `remove_stop`, which send the whole list of stops),
  `format.shadow` (also `shadow_color`) and `format.quickStyle`, each the engine's command with the shapes selected on the page on view. Our rule: **a slider that is dragged shows its value on the page as it
  goes and is one undo step**: each frame of the drag takes back the step the last frame made and makes it again with the new value, while the drawing is as the last frame left it. Home's Quick Styles opens the two grids (the
  tile the first shape has is marked; no live preview: it would be an edit); Fill has Gradient, Pattern and Transparency submenus and Line a Transparency one, and Effects, Shadow has the ten presets and Shadow Options... (the
  Effects tab). **The Format Painter carries the transparency, the pattern, the cap, the compound type, the gradients and the shadow**, and a source with no shadow takes the shadow away from the shape painted, in the same undo step.

## Templates

Starting a drawing from a template, the stencils the template opens, and the list File > New shows. **In the window** (`new_page.rs`) File > New has the headings Personal, Templates and Sample Diagrams: Templates holds the Blank drawing tile and a tile for each template of `template.list`, each showing its name and its category over a picture the window draws from the template's `page` and `theme` (a sheet of the page's proportions with a plain picture of its category, in the first accents of its theme), and its description is the tooltip; a press asks about unsaved changes as Ctrl+N does (`save::Then::Template`) and runs `file.new {template: id}`; Sample Diagrams says there are none (the engine has no samples) and Personal is quiet (the window has no folder of the person's own templates); there is no detail dialog and no Metric or US choice at the start. The rows are (Start a drawing from a template, Template opens its stencils,
Basic Flowchart template, Basic Diagram template: P0; Template sets page scale, size and orientation, Browse templates by category, Save a drawing as a custom template, Start a drawing from a custom template: P1). The format is
a `.vstx` is a drawing under the template content type; the file holds no flag saying it opens as a new document, so the command layer decides and (the
windows part). Code: `crates/engine/src/template/` (`catalogue.rs`, the four templates; `build.rs`, a template made into a document and the bytes of a `.vstx`; `names.rs`, the list in DiaCraft's part that names a template's stencils),
`cmd/template.rs` and `cmd/file.rs`. The reference app was not inspected, and nothing here has been opened in it: every rule below is **ours**. **The templates are originals** (`docs/provenance.md`): four descriptions in code, no file
from another application, nothing traced.

| Command | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `template.list` (query) | none; works with no document | `{templates: [{id, name, category, description, page: {size, size_name, orientation, width, height}, theme: {id, name, variant} or null, stencils: [{id, name}]}], categories}`: the templates in the order File > New lists them (`basic_diagram`, `basic_flowchart`, `cross_functional_flowchart`: its page holds three lanes and three phases, and `organization_chart`: its page holds an Executive), the page each starts (inches), the theme it applies, and the built-in stencils it opens, first shown first. There is no thumbnail: a template's page is empty or holds only what it seeds, and the window draws its card from `page`, `theme` and `stencils` |
| `file.new` | `template`? (an id of `template.list`, in any case, or the name, or the **path** of a template file, `.diacraft-template`, `.vstx` or `.vstm`) | the new document as `document.inspect`; with a template also `template` (`{id, name, builtin: true}`, or `{id: null, name: null, builtin: false}` for a file), `stencils: {open: [ids], ignored}` (the built-in stencils now open, and how many names in the template were not opened), `warnings`, and for a file `file: {variant, kind, bytes, warning_count, skipped_count}`. A blank drawing when `template` is left out, answered as it always was, with every built-in stencil open |
| `file.open` | (as before) | of a template file: the same drawing and the same stencils as `file.new {template: path}` (`stencils: {open, ignored}` is in the answer, for a template only); with `edit_template: true` the file itself, with the stencils it names open. A file that is not a template has every built-in stencil open |

* A built-in template is held in code and written on demand; ours. A template is a `TemplateSpec`: a page size (an id of `page.presets`), an orientation, a theme of the catalogue with its variant (or none), and
  the built-in stencils it opens. `template::build` makes a new drawing (the root style and one page, "Page-1"), gives the page its size and turn by the page setup rules, applies the theme by the theme rules (so the theme's
  part, the `Theme` style and the page's selector cells are in it), writes the list that names its stencils (into DiaCraft's part), and has the one writer (`diacraft_vsdx::write_with`) make the **`.vstx` bytes**, with no times, so the same bytes
  each time. **A built-in template is then read from those bytes by the reader, as a template file is**: there is no second path, and what a test of the built-in templates shows is what any template file does. They are not
  files shipped with the app because the web build has no folder to read them from, and an asset file would need a provenance row and a place to be found. Basic Diagram: Letter, landscape, no theme, the Basic Shapes and Arrow Shapes stencils.
  Basic Flowchart: Letter, portrait, the theme Fernwood, the Basic Flowchart stencil and then the Basic Shapes. Cross-Functional Flowchart: Letter, landscape, no theme, the Cross-Functional Flowchart Shapes, the Basic Flowchart and the Basic Shapes, and three lanes and three phases on its page. Organization Chart: Letter, landscape, no theme, the Organization Chart Shapes and then the Basic Shapes, and an Executive on its page. All four are drawn one to one. Each costs the same whatever drawing it replaces (see "What it costs").
* A template starts a new unsaved drawing; the rule for `file.open`. The document is the template's content, with its page setup, styles and theme; the session keeps **no file's bytes and no
  path** (NP10: the model holds every part of the template), so `file.save` says to use `file.save_as` and the first save is `.diacraft` or, as a VSDX type, written from the model; the drawing is **clean until it is edited**, has no undo step, and is untitled. A template that
  cannot be started (a name that is not a template and is not a path, a drawing or a stencil in its place, a file that cannot be read, one past a limit of the reader) is refused in words and **leaves the open drawing, its
  history and the stencils as they were**. `template` is judged as an id first (in any case, with spaces and hyphens as underscores, or the template's name), and anything else is a path (a word with no `/`, `\` or `.` in it is
  told it is no template, and is not read as a file; `template::is_path` is that rule, and the engine and the window both use it). A path is read as `file.open` reads one (the one reading path, A2 and A5 of the gaps). **`file.new` and `file.open` inside a batch are refused before any work** (each checks first: no template is made and no file is read; a test counts the commits, steps and resolvers and finds none, and the file that would have said something of its own is not looked at). **The window reads a template file on a thread**, as it reads a file to open: `engine.execute file.new {template: path}` on the control channel is answered when the reading ends (`docs/control-protocol.md`), and the window's `new_from_template_file` does the same for a control of its own; a template that is an id is made at once.
* How a template names the stencils it opens; ours; moved into DiaCraft's part. The specs give the format one record of open stencils, the stencil windows of the windows part, whose
  `Document` is a **file path** ("can reveal a user's folder layout"); the model does not keep that part, a built-in stencil has no file to name, and no spec says that a reader
  opens a template's stencil windows (R32 is `unknown`). So **a template's list is a child `stencils` of DiaCraft's own solution part** (`Document::set_diacraft_child`: the part the Snap & Glue
  options live in), whose attribute `open` lists **built-in stencil ids** (`basic`, `flowchart`) separated by `;`, in the order the Shapes window lists them. It is window state and not a cell of the ShapeSheet, where the first
  version of this rule put it (`User.DiaCraftStencils`: the target app would show it in the document's ShapeSheet, copy it into every drawing and never keep it up to date; no release wrote it, so nothing reads it). The target
  app leaves the part alone, and data of a version this build does not read is kept as it is and not interpreted. A template **with** the child opens exactly those (an empty list opens none); a template **without** it
  (every template another application made) opens the stencils a new drawing has; **a list that names stencils and none that can be opened** (a template made for stencils this app does not have) also gets those, and not an
  empty Shapes window. **A name is never followed**: an entry that is not the id of a built-in stencil (a path, a stencil file's `file-1`, a repeat) is counted in `stencils.ignored` and in a warning (counted in `warning_count` too), and
  nothing is read for it, because a template is a file somebody sent and opening what it names would let it choose which of the reader's files are read. **The stencil windows of the windows part are not read for this either** (a
  test puts one that names `/etc/passwd`). Bounded: the first 4 KiB of the text and the first 64 entries are looked at, whatever the attribute holds. **A list that is the stencils a new drawing has is no child** (and a part with nothing
  else in it is no part), so a template with the default stencils is the file it would have been without this rule. A stencil opened from a file stays open when a template narrows the built-in ones (the session's open files are
  not the drawing's).
* Which built-in stencils are open; ours. `stencil.list` lists the built-in stencils that are **open** in the order the Shapes window lists them, then the stencil files, and `available` holds the built-in
  ones that are not. A new session has both open, Basic Shapes first; `file.new` without a template and `file.open` of a file that is not a template **open both again**; a template sets the list to the stencils it names;
  `stencil.open {id}` opens a built-in stencil at the end of the list (a stencil open already says `already_open`), so a drawing from Basic Diagram can open the flowchart stencil from More Shapes. **A stencil that is not open
  still drops by name** (`shape.drop` searches every built-in stencil: a stencil's window is not what a master needs). **`stencil.close` still refuses a built-in stencil**: the window's own rows have no close control, and
  a template decides which are open. The list belongs to the session and goes back with it when a command fails (a failed batch puts it back); it is a plain list of ids, as long as the catalogue. **The Shapes window keys the list it read on the built-in stencils that are open as well as the stencil files**, and forgets it when a new drawing or a file begins, so a drawing from a template lists the stencils the template opened. **A template's answer (it carries `stencils`) also clears the stencil shown**, so the first stencil of the template is the one shown; a blank `file.new` and a file that is not a template leave it as it was, and a template opened as a file counts as another unnamed drawing (Drawing3), as `file.new` does.
* A template keeps the stencils that are open; ours; the P1 row "Save a drawing as a custom template". `file.save_as` or `file.save` to a template (`.vstx` or `.vstm`) writes the built-in stencils open at
  that moment into the list in DiaCraft's part **when they are not the ones the drawing's document already implies** (its own list, or, with none, both stencils in the catalogue's order): the list goes into **the copy that is
  written and not into the drawing**, which stays as it was (not dirty, no undo step), and **a list that is the stencils a new drawing has takes the child out of the copy and the part with it when nothing else is in it** (a
  template that had the part and has nothing to say any more is saved without it). So a template opened to be changed and saved again with its stencils as they opened, **whoever made it, is written as it was** with only
  `docProps/app.xml` rewritten (the round trip of `diacraft-cli roundtrip`), a template with no list is not given one for nothing, and a template saved after stencils were opened by hand (`stencil.open {id}`) has them in its list.
  **A document whose DiaCraft data is of a version this build does not read** is saved with that data as it was and the stencils are not recorded: the answer's `warnings` says so first. Stencils opened from files are **not kept**,
  because a template never names a path. A drawing made from a template keeps the list it came with in its own document, so it is saved with it as a drawing; opening that drawing does not open the stencils (only a template does).
* A template may put a shape on its page; ours. A `TemplateSpec` has a `seed`: Cross-Functional Flowchart makes its lanes and phases by the lanes' commands, and Organization Chart drops one Executive (`orgchart.executive`) in the middle of the page, an inch below its top edge, by the function `shape.drop` uses, before the page is written, so that the drawing has a chart, the Org Chart tab shows, and there is a manager to drop positions on (see "The Organization Chart"). The Executive is an instance of a master, so that template holds that one master; Basic Diagram, Basic Flowchart and Cross-Functional Flowchart hold none.
* A template file from another application; ours. It opens, and its stencils are those a new drawing has. It is not given a list by a save that leaves its stencils as they opened.
* **No theme is applied to a template that has none**: Basic Diagram is the plain start, and a shape dropped on its page follows no style until a theme is applied (`theme.apply`). A shape dropped on Basic Flowchart follows
  Fernwood.

## Snap, grid and guides

Snapping is one pure function, `diacraft_engine::snap::snap`, which the commands below and the window's drags both call, and the Snap and Glue settings the document holds. The rows
are snap on or off: P0; the choice of targets, guides, glue to guides: P1; snap strength: P2, "Dynamic
grid" (P1); the cells are `Ruler & Grid`, `InhibitSnap`, the page margins, `NoSnap`, `InfiniteLine`
(`DocumentSettings`). Code: `crates/engine/src/snap/` (`mod.rs` the function, `settings.rs`, `grid.rs`, `index.rs`, `page.rs`, `geometry.rs`, `guides.rs`, and `osnap.rs`, the object snaps on kurbo paths ported from CADCraft, which knows no document type), `cmd/snapping.rs` and `cmd/snap_params.rs`; the words of the
settings are `crates/doc/src/snap_words.rs` and `crates/vsdx/src/write/settings.rs`, and DiaCraft's part is `crates/doc/src/diacraft_data.rs` and `crates/vsdx/src/write/diacraft.rs`. The reference app was not inspected: every rule below is **ours** unless a specification is named, and is listed in the report of the task for the check list.
Every length is in **paper inches**, as in the other editing commands; the page's cells (drawing units) are divided by `draw::units_per_inch` once, in `snap/grid.rs` and `snap/page.rs`.

| Command | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `snap.settings` (query) | none | Every option as a boolean, under its name: the switches `snap` and `glue`; the flags of `SnapSettings`: `ruler`, `grid`, `guides`, `snap_handles`, `snap_vertices`, `snap_connection_points`, `snap_geometry`, `alignment_box`, `snap_extensions`, `intersections`; of `GlueSettings`: `glue_guides`, `glue_handles`, `glue_vertices`, `glue_connection_points`, `glue_geometry`; of `SnapExtensions`: `ext_alignment_box`, `ext_centre_axes`, `ext_tangents`, `ext_segment_ends`, `ext_segment_midpoints`, `ext_linear`, `ext_curved`, `ext_perpendicular_end`, `ext_perpendicular_mid`, `ext_horizontal_end`, `ext_vertical_end`, `ext_ellipse_centres`, `ext_isometric`; and `dynamic_grid` (`DynamicGridEnabled`); the six snap strengths in screen pixels, `strength_ruler`, `strength_grid`, `strength_guides`, `strength_points`, `strength_geometry` and `strength_extensions`, whole numbers from 0 to 999 (4, 5, 10, 10, 8 and 13 at first); and `words`: `SnapSettings`, `GlueSettings`, `SnapExtensions`, `DynamicGridEnabled` as the document holds them (`null` for one it lacks, cut to 64 characters). Only `snap` and `glue` and the strengths are DiaCraft's own; every other option is a flag of the file's own word |
| `snap.set` | any of the thirty-one options of `snap.settings`, booleans, and any of its six strengths, whole numbers of screen pixels from 0 to 999 | The settings as `snap.settings`. The drawing is changed (unsaved) and **no undo step is made**; the revision moves once, so what the snap read for the old options is read again. Naming nothing, or what is already so, changes nothing at all. A strength that is not a whole number from 0 to 999 (negative, 1000, a fraction, text, a boolean) is refused in words that name it and the range (`bad params`), and the command changes nothing. Refused (`failed`, nothing changed) when an option's word is text that is not a whole number or a boolean, or the file's DiaCraft data is of a version this build does not change |
| `snap.at` (query) | `page`, `x` and `y` (a place) or `left`, `bottom`, `right` and `top` (a box), `ids`? (the shapes that move: never targets, and with a box their connection points are pulled with it, where the box would put them), `connect`? (the place is the end of a connector), `zoom`? (screen points to an inch, 96), and the words of the commands: `edges`?, `keep`?, `lock`?, `nudge`?, `view`?, `rotate`? (below) | `{dx, dy, hits, marks, glue, holds, truncated, examined}` and the place or box after the snap (`x`, `y`, or `left`, `bottom`, `right`, `top`); `holds` are the guides a box would be held to, each `{guide, axis, anchor, cell}`, and nothing is written |
| `guide.add` | `page`, `orientation` (`vertical` or `horizontal`), `at` (inches from the left or bottom edge) | `{page, id, orientation, at}` |
| `guide.move` | `id`, `at`, `page`? | `{page, id, at, moved}`; the shapes held to the guide follow it |
| `guide.delete` | `id`, `page`? | `{page, id}`; refused (`failed`) while a `Connect` row or an alignment cell of the page names the guide |
| `guide.list` (query) | `page`? | `{page, guides: [{id, orientation, at}], left_out}` |

`shape.drop`, `shape.move`, `shape.resize`, `shape.place`, `connector.add` and `connector.reconnect` take **`snap`**: `true`, or `{zoom}` (`shape.move` also takes `lock`, below); left out, `false` or `null` is no snapping, as before. The result then holds `snap`, the answer of the function (`dx`, `dy`,
`hits`: each `{target, axis, normal?, line, anchor, shape?, point?, span?, gap?}` (a hit on a line along no page axis has no `axis` and a `normal`) with the `target` one of `grid`, `ruler`, `guide`, `alignment`, `page_margin`, `spacing`, `connection_point`, `vertex`, `handle`, `outline`, `intersection`, `glue`, and `holds`: each `{guide, axis, anchor, cell}`),
and for `shape.drop`, `shape.move`, `shape.resize` and `shape.place` also `held`: the `ID`s of the shapes the command leaves held to the guides the snap pulled them to (a hold it wrote, or one the edit wrote again where the shape is); a command that changed nothing (a shape placed where it is, already held so) names none.
The document's own Snap switch off (`snap.set {snap: false}`) makes `snap` do nothing. **A zoom is a number above 0 at which the strongest pull is a distance on the page**: `snap.zoom` and the `zoom` of `snap.at` refuse, in words, a zoom so small that a pull of 999 points (the strongest strength) is more inches than the largest number, below about 5.6E-306 points to an inch, where the reach of an alignment was no number and nothing lined up (`docs/gaps.md` F36); every zoom at which an alignment works is taken, as before.

**Snapping through the shared library.** The snapping function gives the page's targets to `craft-snap` and tells what it gives in the same words as before (`hits`, `holds`, `glue`). The policy is data in `snap/adapter.rs`: as priorities, places before lines, a pull of 12, 8, 8 and 6 points, and, from the adoption, a side of a box lines up with the same side of another shape only (a guide, a page margin and the grid take any side), a box lines up with shapes anywhere on the page (the mark runs to the shape), equal spacing offers any gap of the row (the row is the shapes whose boxes share a band across the axis), a shape whose `LinePattern` is 0 offers no alignment lines (and stays in equal-gap rows), and far drawings snap as near ones do. **`lock`** (`shape.move`): `snap: {lock: "x"}` (or `"y"`) keeps the move to that page axis: the other axis moves not at all, to the bit, whatever `dx` or `dy` says, and the free axis snaps to everything a free move snaps to (guides, other shapes, margins, spacing, the grid) and is held to a guide it lands on; a pull on the locked axis is ignored; a connection point, vertex or outline pulls it where it lies on a line the box travels along; any other value is refused in words. The checks: `crates/engine/tests/it/snap_adoption.rs` (the spec's vectors), and the harness in `crates/engine/src/snap/equal.rs`.

**The words of `snap` the adoption adds** (the window sends them, and `snap.at` and every editing command with `snap` tell the same `marks`): `lock` (`shape.move`: `"x"` or `"y"`, the move keeps to that page axis), `nudge: {tick}` (`shape.move`: one press of an arrow key, `dx` and `dy` giving the direction, one of them 0, `tick` inches when nothing is ahead, else the ruler tick at the zoom), `view: [x0, y0, x1, y1]` (`shape.resize`: the window's visible part of the page in inches, so a width or height stops at that of a shape in it, every indexed shape when it is left out), `keep: "proportions"` (`shape.resize`: a corner keeps the shape's proportions, a side keeps the opposite side and the other axis grows about its middle) and `radius` (`shape.rotate`, with `angle`: how far the pointer is from the pin in inches, for how far another shape's angle pulls and for the rotation handle's step). Every `snap` result carries `marks`: lines with the shapes they run to, equal gaps with their lengths and chains, sizes and angles, each with its label, and `landed` (a resize that keeps proportions) and `angle` (a turn). A `view` that is not four numbers, a `keep` other than `"proportions"`, a `nudge` with two directions or a `tick` of 0, and a turn with no `radius` or by an amount are refused in words. **Each command takes the words it has:** `zoom` and `view` are every snapping command's (the window sends the view with every one, and only a resize reads it), `lock` and `nudge` are `shape.move`'s, `keep` is `shape.resize`'s and `radius` is `shape.rotate`'s, and a word of another command, a drop sent `lock` for one, is refused in words that name the words the command takes. A `view` of four numbers and something else is refused, and so is a resize with `keep` of a shape whose proportions are a ratio of millions, which would take the box past the page. **`snap.at` takes the subjects and words of the commands:** `edges` (an array of `left`, `bottom`, `right` and `top`: the box is a resize and the answer's box is the one it comes to, `landed` with `keep`), `keep` (`"proportions"`, with `edges` and the one shape in `ids`), `lock` (`"x"` or `"y"`, with a place or a box), `nudge` (`{direction, tick}` with a box, `direction` being `right`, `left`, `up` or `down`: one press of an arrow key, and `dx` and `dy` are the press), `view` and `rotate` (`{angle, radius}` with one id and no place or box: a turn, its `angle`, `hits` and `marks` those of `shape.rotate`). `shape.place` and `snap.at` align a turned shape by the box round its paths, as `shape.move` does, and `snap.at` carries the own points of `ids` by how far the box it is given is from that box. **What `truncated` says:** also that a turned target is lined up by the box round its corners because the page budget could not pay for the box round its paths. A call's outline budget is its distinct shapes, and a call that asks the library again tells the marks of each axis from the query that found its hits. The counted checks of the work one call does are the call's, not each library query's: a box or a point is asked up to three times (each axis alone, the outlines apart) and the points, pieces, pairs, outlines and connection rows are one budget across them. **A turned shape lines up by the box round its paths**: a shape turned by other than whole quarter turns, target or moving, has the page-axis lines of the level and upright box round the paths it draws (a section with `NoShow` 1 does not count), a turned group the box round its members' paths, and a picture the box round its corners. The page makes its targets' boxes once, under a page budget (2 MiB of constructor text, 65,536 points, 262,144 path elements and 80 million of spline work, over all its turned shapes), and `shape.move` makes the moving shapes' box (`snap::bounds_of`, the same function a window's preview calls) under one query's budget for the whole selection; a shape the budget cannot pay for, one that draws no path and a group of more than 256 shapes keep the box round their corners, and the results of `shape.move`, `shape.place` and `snap.at` say in `snap.corner_boxes` how many shapes did. A resize still meets page lines through its width-height box. **Turned shapes snap by their own sides (spec):** one shape turned by other than whole quarter turns that `shape.move`, `shape.place` or `snap.at` snaps lines up its own sides (its frame: the width and height as drawn, the direction of its own x axis from its cells) as well as the box round its paths: its own sides, along up to four directions, meet the like sides of shapes turned the same way or a quarter, half or three-quarter turn from it (a low side meets a low side, a middle a middle), and a guide along no axis that is parallel to one of them; a snap to such a guide is not held. A point, an unturned box and the end of a connector are told no slanted guide, and a shape turned by another angle, even a billionth of a radian off, offers its own lines to none. A hit on such a line has no `axis` and a `normal` (the angle of the line's normal in degrees: a side of a shape turned 30 degrees along its own y axis has a normal of 120, and runs along 30), a mark of it has `axis` null and the same `normal`, and a row of shapes turned alike has its gaps marked along its own direction. A turned subject is asked of the library once, so that what is drawn is where it lands: two page lines each within reach but farther than the larger reach together are not both taken. A `shape.resize` of a shape that is turned or flipped is snapped in its own axes (`x` and `y` are the corner at its own (0, 0), `width` and `height` run along its own axes): the sides that move are worked out in them, a flipped shape's handle being the side it is on, a corner or the middle of a moving side meets a line of the page, an own side meets the like side of a shape turned alike, a width or height meets another's, `keep` keeps its own proportions, the angle and the sides that stay are unchanged, and the box it comes to is `snap.frame` (`centre`, `width`, `height`, `angle` in degrees). No hold is written for a turned or flipped shape; one whose corners on the page are no rectangle, or that is a member of a turned group, is not snapped. **The library's three settings:** a place on a line one of a box's places travels along pulls a move held to an axis (`lock`) and the box lands on it, the nearest place stops an arrow key whatever its kind, and a line mark runs from the nearest shape on one side of the box to the nearest on the other. `snap.at` refuses `connect`, which is the end of a connector, beside a box, `edges`, `nudge`, `keep` and `rotate`, in words.

* **Where the settings live.** The format publishes the flags of `SnapSettings` (what a shape snaps to: ruler subdivisions 1, grid 2, guides 4, handles 8, vertices 16,
  connection points 32, outlines 256, alignment box 512, extensions 1024, intersections 65536), of `GlueSettings` (what a connector's end glues to: guides 1, handles 2, vertices 4, connection points 8, geometry 32) and of `SnapExtensions` (thirteen
  extension lines, 1 to 4096), and R56 says `DynamicGridEnabled` is a boolean. **So each of those options is the file's own**, one flag of its word (`snap/settings.rs` has the table): a file reads each option from the bit of its word when the word is a whole
  number (any size an `i64` holds), and from the boolean `true`, `false`, `1` or `0`; and **a change sets or clears that option's flag and nothing else of the word**: every bit the file holds that the table does not name (64 and 128 of `SnapSettings`, 16 of
  `GlueSettings`, a bit past 32, a negative value) and the flag 32768 are kept as read. A word the file lacks is made from the flags of the settings when one of its options changes (65847 for `SnapSettings` at the defaults); a word the file lacks and no
  option of which changed is not made. A word that is text and not a whole number (or a boolean) is left alone, and **a change to one of its options is refused** (L2). `DynamicGridEnabled` is written `1` or `0`; a literal that already says the value (`true`) stays.
  **A word is the element of the file's own namespace**: a `SnapSettings` that declares `xmlns="urn:x"` (or a prefix bound to a namespace of another application on the element itself) is another application's, and is neither read nor written by the model (`diacraft_doc::is_other_namespace`, one rule); where the model and the writer, which resolves every prefix, would not mean the same element (a prefix bound on the root), nothing of the words is written and the save warns, and the baseline it returns holds the file's words so that the next save says so again. A new drawing carries the empty `SnapAngles` between `SnapExtensions` and `DynamicGridEnabled` that every sample holds. The words are changed in the file's bytes (`vsdx/src/write/settings.rs` `patch_bytes`): **a save that changes one option changes the text of that one word and no other byte of the document part**, and no byte of `[Content_Types].xml`; a word that is added,
  taken out, written with a reference or with Unicode blanks round it (the bytes are read back and must be the tree the words were patched into) is written from the tree. **The windows part's copies are not touched**: a window's `SnapSettings` of 17 stays 17 when the document's 773 becomes 775; which copy the reference app applies when they differ is not known, and DiaCraft reads
  and writes the document's alone.
  **What stays in DiaCraft's part** (`Document::diacraft`): the **Snap and Glue switches**, `snap` and `glue`. R55 reads the flag 32768 of the two words as "snapping off" and "glue off", but no page says the dialog's own Snap and Glue
  boxes (`Document.SnapEnabled`, `GlueEnabled`) are stored as that flag, so they stay ours until that is confirmed: a child `snap` with the attributes `snap` and `glue`, 1 or 0, written when either is off and taken out when both are on; the flag 32768 is neither
  read as the switch nor set nor cleared. `vsdx/src/write/diacraft.rs` writes the part: it patches the `Data` element of a part the file has, adds the `Solution` row to a solutions index another writer made, or makes the index, the part, their relationships and
  types through the writer's own content types; another writer's solutions are not touched; and **the writer replaces only the data the reader read** (a `Data` with no `version`, with more items than the document keeps, or in a part over 4 MiB is left as it
  is and the save warns), and none of that fails a save. A part with nothing left is removed with its relationship, its type and its row, and the index when it holds nothing else.
  **A file saved by ** keeps the other options in that child (and made only flags 1 and 2 of `SnapSettings`). Where the word of an option is there, **the word wins** and the part's value is ignored; where the word is absent or is not a number, the part's value is read.
  The next change of any option writes the child again with the two switches only, and the values that were the part's alone for a word the file lacks are written into that word first, so none is lost (and a value for a word that is text, which cannot be written, stays in the part). (A `SnapSettings` word that made from nothing holds
  flags 1 and 2 alone, and now reads the other options of that word as off.) A part of another version reads as the defaults and is not changed.
* **Defaults.** They are what a new drawing is written with (`file.new` and the built-in templates give the four words `SnapSettings` 65847, `GlueSettings` 9, `SnapExtensions` 34 and `DynamicGridEnabled` 1, as most files saved by the reference app
  do) and what a word, or a file, that gives none reads as. **Where a page documents a default it is that; where it does not it is the value most sample files hold for that flag, which is ours:** each is a count flag by flag over the samples, a statistic and not the values of any one file (asks that a skeleton's values not be copied from a sample, and none is). Documented: `SnapExtensions` is 34 (centre axes and
  linear extension: `Document.SnapExtensions`; 1,885 files), and the rulers and the grid are on ("Rulers and grid are on by default"). From the samples: snap to guides 1,850 files, to connection points 1,893, to outlines 1,842,
  to vertices and to intersections 1,166 each (61%), glue to guides 1,865 and to connection points 1,898, the dynamic grid 1,704 (89%) — all on; snap to handles (23), the alignment box (19), extensions (26), glue to handles (22), vertices (23) and geometry (27) — off.
  The switches are on (ours). So a placing is pulled by connection points, vertices, outlines and crossings by default, and a connector's end by guides and connection points.
* **The grid and the ruler's subdivisions** (`snap/grid.rs`): a fixed grid: the origin plus multiples of the spacing at any zoom, spacing 0: no line, and for a variable grid, **in drawing inches, divided into paper inches**: an
  eighth times a power of two, the smallest that leaves the lines 16 points apart at density 8 (32 at 4, 64 at 2) at the zoom, never closer than a spacing above 0. At Letter or A4 the lines are 0.25 of paper at 1:1, 0.32 at 1:100 and 1/6 at a quarter inch to the
  foot (at 96 points to an inch). The ruler follows: ticks from the ruler's zero in the ruler's unit (`DrawingScale`'s `U`). The window draws these lines, so that what a drag snaps to is what is drawn.
* A target pulls from as many screen **pixels** as its kind's strength, the six numbers of the Advanced tab's sliders, which are **the
  document's settings** (ours: `snap.set` sets them and `snap.settings` answers them, and they are saved with the drawing, below): Rulers (`strength_ruler`, ruler subdivisions) 4, Grid (`strength_grid`) 5, Guides (`strength_guides`) 10, Points (`strength_points`: connection points, vertices, handles) 10, Geometry (`strength_geometry`: outlines and intersections) 8 and Extensions
  (`strength_extensions`) 13, each from 0 to 999, at the zoom of the query (`craft-snap`'s `Reach::Screen`: a target pulls if and only if its distance on the screen is within its strength, so 0 pulls only what lies on it). **Extensions is the dynamic grid's** ("adjust the Extensions snap strength"): the alignment of other shapes, the page
  margins, equal spacings and equal sizes pull from it (Q4 leaves open which slider the alignment box's own snap uses, and there are no extension lines yet); a turn's match of another shape's angle keeps its 6 pixels (no page gives a turn a strength). **This changed behaviour:** the grid and the ruler no longer pull from any distance (the nearest line, as the window snapped before), so a shape more than 5 pixels from a grid line and
  4 from a ruler tick lands where it was put; and the defaults are the specification's, so points pull from 10 pixels (were 12), guides from 10 (were 8), and the alignment, margins and spacing from 13 (were 6). The window's drags read the same settings (the page's cache is by revision, so a change shows at once). Where several pull, **the stronger kind wins over a nearer weaker one**: connection point, vertex, handle, intersection, guide, outline, alignment/margin/spacing, grid, ruler. A
  place that is a target (connection point, vertex, handle, outline, intersection) pulls both axes; a line pulls one.
* **What moves and what snaps.** A **drop** is dropped and then moved by the snap of its box (centre ± the master's size), in the same undo step, and is never a target of its own snap. A **move** snaps the joint box of the shapes named, where the move would put it (at most 512 shapes with `snap`; more is
  refused), by its three anchors on each axis (low edge, middle, high edge), the nearest of the strongest kind. A **resize** snaps only the edges that differ from the shape's box now (the middle never), and a shape that is turned or flipped is snapped along its own axes (below), one whose corners on the page are no rectangle not at all. A connector end given as `{x, y}` snaps as a place: **onto a connection point it becomes the glue to it**
  (`{id, point}`); onto a vertex, a handle or an outline it lands there and stays a free end (glue to a point of an outline has no cell form here yet).
* **Our rule (gap F12).** The specifications give the options of Snap to (`SnapSettings` 8, 16, 32, 256 and 65536) and Glue to (`GlueSettings` 2, 4, 8 and 32) as what pulls "shapes", and say nothing of what a shape being dropped, moved or resized is pulled to. We take them to pull the shape's **places** to the same places of other shapes (a pointer is one place; a box is nine, its four corners, four middles and centre, and so are the connection points of the shapes that move, at most 32), by **the options of `SnapSettings` (`snap_handles`, `snap_vertices`, `snap_connection_points`, `snap_geometry`, `intersections`) and the Snap switch** (a connector's end is pulled by the options of `GlueSettings` and the Glue switch; and a placing is not pulled under `InhibitSnap`): its connection points to theirs, its places to their vertices and handles (a corner is both) and to the nearest point of their outlines, and to the crossings of two of their outlines. A place that is a target pulls both axes and wins over a line, and nothing is glued by it (`glue` stays null: a drop is not a connection). A resize pulls its moved edges to lines only. The targets are the object snaps of CADCraft (`snap/osnap.rs`: endpoint, nearest, intersection, with the score that lets an endpoint or an intersection win a near tie by 15% of the pull; tangent and perpendicular need a base point, which a drag has not, and nothing asks for them), applied to the outlines `diacraft_geom` builds from a shape's `Geometry` rows. **A consequence:** `snap_connection_points`, `snap_vertices`, `snap_geometry` and `intersections` are on by default, so a placing with the default settings reads the shapes near it, as it did not before. **Two more, ours:** a place that is a target pulls both axes at once and returns before the lines are weighed, so a box near the connection point of a neighbour takes that pull and not the grid's on either axis; and only a shape's box, its connection points and the outlines that its `Geometry` sections draw are places: a path that lies outside the shape's box is not a target (the shape is looked for by its box).
* **The dynamic grid.** With `alignment_box` or `dynamic_grid` the edges and middles of the shapes within 2 inches (the nearest 64) are lines; `dynamic_grid` adds the page margins (`PageLeftMargin` and its three: 0.25 where a page holds none) and **spacing**: a gap to the shape beside the subject equal to the gap on its other side, or to the gap between that shape and the one beyond
  it (the shapes within 4 inches; shapes in another row or column are not neighbours). Each hit says how far the aid reaches (`span`) and the equal gap.
* **`InhibitSnap`.** A page with `InhibitSnap` 1 snaps to its ruler and grid and to nothing else of the page (guides, other shapes, spacing); a connection point is glue and stays. **`NoSnap`** 1 on a `Geometry` section leaves its path out of the vertices, outlines and intersections. The members of a group are not targets (the group is one box: `IsSnapTarget` read as 0).
* A guide is a page shape of `Type="Guide"` with one `Geometry` section holding the `InfiniteLine` row (`X`, `Y` 0, `A` 1, `B` 0), `Angle` 0 or a quarter turn, `Width` and `Height` 0, and `NonPrinting` and the three `ShapePermeable` cells 1 on the shape (a new drawing has no guide style to give them). The renderer leaves it out; a page's extents do not count it. A guide
  read from a file counts when its line lies along an axis (a tilt under a thousandth of a radian); another is left out and `left_out` counts it. `guide.move` moves the pin by the difference, so a guide from a file moves as it is. A guide's `Angle` is written to thirteen places (`1.5707963267949` for an upright one), as an angle is in a file: at nine the line is tilted
  by 3E-10, which a shape held to it far up the page follows. Glue to a guide is the rule **Glue to a guide** below, and a guide that shapes are held to is not deleted by `guide.delete`. Connector routing must not take a guide for an obstacle (it holds `ShapePermeable*` 1).
* **Glue to a guide (`snap/hold.rs`).** **When it glues.** A box being placed (`shape.drop`, `shape.move`, `shape.resize`, `shape.place`, with `snap`; and `snap.at`, which only tells) that the snap pulls to a guide on an axis, with the
  Glue switch and `glue_guides` on (and so with `guides` on and no `InhibitSnap`, without which no guide pulls a placing), is held to that guide: `Snapped::holds` names it, at most one on each axis, and the command holds each shape that moves whose **own** edge or middle on that axis is on the guide (within a millionth of an inch), so of
  several shapes moved together only those are held (ours). A shape is held only if it is a top-level 2-D shape, not a guide, not turned or flipped (the forms have neither) and with no `GUARD` on its pins or alignment cells; a turned shape is pulled and not held. A guide whose line misses its pin by more than a millionth of an inch (a file's guide
  whose local pin is off its line) holds nothing: the formulas name the pin and would put the shape off the line it snapped to. **What is written**, in the command's one undo step:
  the **glued cell** of the axis and the part of the box pulled (`AlignLeft`, `AlignCenter`, `AlignRight` for an upright guide; `AlignBottom`, `AlignMiddle`, `AlignTop` for a level one) is `INTERSECTX` (x cells) or `INTERSECTY` (y cells) of `Sheet.<g>!PinX,Sheet.<g>!PinY,Sheet.<g>!Angle`, the shape's place as two drawing lengths (`4 DL,1.5 DL`) and
  `Sheet.<g>!Angle+90 deg`; the **other axis's middle cell** (`AlignMiddle` for an upright guide, `AlignCenter` for a level one) is the other function of the same arguments; at a **crossing** of an upright guide `a` and a level guide `b` the x cell and the y cell are each the function of `Sheet.<a>!PinX,Sheet.<a>!PinY,Sheet.<a>!Angle,Sheet.<b>!PinX,Sheet.<b>!PinY,Sheet.<b>!Angle`
  (the upright guide first in both: ours, the spec asks for one order); the **four other cells** are `_MARKER(1)` with `V` 1 and no `U`; every guide cell has a numeric `V` and no `U`; **`PinX`** becomes `LocPinX+AlignLeft`, `LocPinX+AlignCenter-Width/2` or `LocPinX+AlignRight-Width`, and **`PinY`** `LocPinY+AlignBottom`, `LocPinY+AlignMiddle-Height/2` or
  `LocPinY+AlignTop-Height`, following the cell of its axis, keeping their unit; and **one `Connect` row for each glued cell**: `FromCell` the cell, `FromPart` 1 to 6 (left, centre, right, bottom, middle, top), `ToSheet` the guide, `ToCell` `Angle`, `ToPart` 4; the middle cell of a hold to one guide has none. A shape's rows from alignment cells are replaced
  whole; a shape held already as the placing would hold it is not written again, and the result's `held` names the shapes whose hold the command wrote. **The place** in the one-guide form is the middle of the shape's box where it came to rest (drawing units, in the page's frame): a reading of ("the shape's own centre or middle"), ours
  Is the same point for a shape whose pin is in its middle, and the middle is what keeps any other shape still under `LocPinY+AlignMiddle-Height/2`. It changes no value for an upright or level guide, and is where a shape goes when its guide turns.
  **Following**: a write to a guide's `PinX`, `PinY` or `Angle` (`guide.move`; `shape.move` or `shape.rotate` of the guide; any cell write) is worked out with what reads it, so the guide cells and then the pins move; the rows are not read for it. **The shapes a guide carries count as changed by the edit**, so the connectors glued to them are
  placed again (an end glued to the whole of a shape included) and routed again. A guide turned (our reasoning) carries its shapes to the foot of the line square to it through the place (a level guide through (0, 0) turned to 45 degrees takes a shape held at (2, 0) to (1, 1)); a turned guide is no snap target and is
  not listed, and its shapes stay held. A resize with `snap` keeps each hold of the shape where the part of the box held is still on its guide after it (an edge it does not move, and a middle that stays, as in a resize by both edges alike: ours), and a move with `snap` along the guide renews the hold at the new place. **`shape.size` moves a held shape's pin**:
  it keeps the pin's formula, so the held edge stays on the guide and the pin follows the new size (held by its right edge at 5 and made 3 wide, a box's pin goes from 4 to 3.5). **Auto Size**: a page that grows on the left or the bottom does not write the pins of shapes held whole to guides, since the guides it carries carry them; a shape
  held to one guide has its place written moved with the page, so that its free axis is carried too.
  **What is read and kept and never written**: the share form (shares of the `Width` and `Height` of the sheet a shape is in), a shape with a single guide cell and no row, and a line's or connector's end on a guide (`BeginX` and the others calling `INTERSECTX`/`INTERSECTY`, with rows `FromPart` 7, 8, 10 and 11): a
  file's are opened, followed and saved as they are (a move of such a line keeps its glued ends: MOVE-1D), and a connector's end that `snap` pulls onto a guide lands on it as a free place. `_MARKER` is worked out by `calc` (it gives back its argument as a plain number, the project rule).
* **Releasing a guide (the specification: "a write breaks the hold"; ours where it is silent).** A **guide cell** is an alignment cell whose own formula names a shape with `Sheet.<id>!`, whatever the call round the guide's (`INTERSECTX(...)`, `GUARD(INTERSECTX(...))`, `INTERSECTX(...)+0`). After any edit (`draw::settle`, and `settle_kept` for a group, a paste, a duplicate and a page that grows), a shape whose `PinX`, `PinY`, `Angle`, `FlipX` or `FlipY` the edit wrote is judged:
  **released whole** when it is now turned (by other than a half turn) or flipped (such a shape is never held), or turned by a half turn with its pin off its box's middle (the forms then name the wrong edge); **kept** while each pin still reads a cell of its axis that is a guide cell or that a row is from; else **written again**
  where the shape is now, its rows kept, when every axis whose pin no longer reads its cell is the **free axis of a hold to one guide** (the axis of the middle cell that has no row, `shape.place {y}` on a left-edge hold) or a **glued axis whose edge is still on its guide's line**, within a millionth of an inch (a pin written back on its own line,
  as the arrow key's nudge `shape.move {dx: 0, dy: step}` writes `PinX`; at a crossing a write that keeps both edges on their lines keeps it too); and else **released whole**: a glued pin written off its line, and so a crossing written off either line (ours). Released, its rows from alignment cells go, and each of its guide cells is written as the value it holds, with no
  formula (its unit as it was: none) (the spec does not say what an unglued shape holds: **we keep the value**); a guarded guide cell is left as a drag leaves a guarded cell; the marker cells stay, and a pin's formula that still reads a cell reads a value now, so the shape does not move. So a move, a place or a resize without `snap` that writes a glued axis
  off its line, a move with `snap` not pulled to the guide, a turn and a flip each release it, and a nudge along the guide, a re-pin that leaves the held edge on its line and a half turn of a shape pinned in its middle do not. A shape whose glued cell is guarded is released where it would be written again. **Copies**: every copy of a held shape is released, put
  elsewhere or where its original is (no form of is two guide cells with no row, and a copy has no row of its own); a guide copied with its shape releases the copy too. **Grouping** a guide releases the shapes held to it first, while its pin is the page's, so they stay where they are. **Deleting**: a held shape's rows go with it
  Every shape that goes (a guide deleted as a shape by `shape.delete` or `edit.cut`, or whatever shape a file's alignment cells name) releases the shapes held to it (an alignment cell of theirs names it: every glued cell names its row's guide), in one walk of the page however many go, and they stay where they are: every alignment cell of theirs that names a
  shape that goes is made the constant of its value, `GUARD` and all (`remap::freeze_refs_of`, as a copy's cells naming shapes not copied are), and so is every alignment cell of a copy; and `guide.delete` refuses a guide that a row or an alignment cell of the page names (a file's shape with a guide cell and no row, as the ten shapes hold one).
  **Cost**: a guide's move finds the shapes it carries in one walk of the page, and the settling of the edit finds them, their connectors and the shapes those are glued to in a few walks of the page in all (`scan::Located`: one map from `ID` to place for the settling, and a shape looked up once is not looked for again), not one search each (1,003,003 shapes visited by the settling's searches for 1,000 held; now one walk of the page of 1,001 shapes and the search for the guide, 1,002, besides the walk that finds the shapes carried).
* **Cost.** The grid, the ruler and the guides cost arithmetic and one pass over the page's top-level shapes for the guides (`SnapPage::read`: 100 microseconds for 12,000; a guide past the 1,000th is counted and not read). The targets that are other shapes use an index of the page made once, **bounded at 5,000 top-level shapes** (`snap/index.rs`: 26 ms in the development profile for 5,000, once for a drag, or for a command that asks for those targets), and a query looks at the shapes in the buckets near the subject, at most 64
  of them: 160 looked at on a page of 12,000 as on a page of 2,000, about 4 microseconds. **Whatever the page holds the work has a cap, and a cap that is met sets `truncated`:** a gather of the index visits at most 4,096 bucket entries (a shape in several buckets once for each; a bitmap, not a sort, says a shape was seen; **the buckets are visited from the middle of the window outward**, in rings, so that when the visits run out it is the shapes farthest from the pointer that are not looked at, and a shape that covers more than 16 buckets is in a list that is looked at first, none is left out, and each of its entries is a visit too) and a query makes at most three gathers (`MAX_GATHERS`: the region, and the spacing along each axis); at most 4,096 target points and 16,384 pieces of outline are weighed, from at most 16 outlines, 64 pairs of them for the crossings; and **the rows a section holds are counted over the shape, its master and the styles before any is merged** (`Resolver::effective_rows_within`): a `Connection` section of more than 256 rows, a `Geometry` section of more than 400 (at most 8 sections), and a guide's of more than 8, are skipped, not read, and the query is `truncated`. **Those rows are charged to the query, not only to the shape:** the rows of the `Geometry` sections the query reads come out of its 4,096 points (counted over the layers and charged before they are merged, so a query merges no more rows than that, however many shapes it looks into), and the rows of `Connection` sections out of 1,024 for the query (`MAX_CONNECTION_ROWS_PER_QUERY`, and 1,024 for the shapes that move); a shape the budget cannot pay for is a target by its box only. **The sections are counted before they are merged as well** (`Resolver::effective_sections_within`): a shape that holds more than 64 `Geometry` sections over its master and the styles (a guide: more than 16) is not listed, so a master of thousands of sections costs a count for each of its instances and no merge (`diacraft_doc::work::sections_merged`). A shape's box is tested against the pull before its points are read. **What the rows hold is bounded too:** a query has one budget for building the outlines of all the shapes it looks into, charged before each `Geometry` section is parsed or built: 128 KiB of constructor text (the cells of `PolylineTo` and `NURBSTo` rows, charged by length), 4,096 points (the rows, and the points those constructors hold, counted in the text), 16,384 path elements and 5 million multiplications of spline work (`diacraft_geom::Bound`); a section the budget cannot pay for is left out and the query is `truncated`. **A curve is flattened only when it is cheap:** from the box of its control points, a segment with a point past a million inches or not a number, one that cannot reach the pointer's aperture, one estimated at more than 4,096 pieces (`2 sqrt(size / 0.001) + 2`), and the estimates past what is left of the query's 16,384 pieces, are left out before they are flattened (the call is given what is left) (`Snapped::flattened` counts the estimates charged; a curve a trillion inches across makes none). 64 instances of a master of 100,000 rows are 0 rows merged for a query (they were 12.8 million). `tests/it/snap.rs` and `tests/it/snap_commands.rs` hold this to counts (`Snapped::examined`, `visited`, `points`, `flattened`, `outline`, `ShapeIndex::read`, `diacraft_doc::work::rows_merged` and `sections_merged`), not times. The index is not kept between commands.

**The window (the Snap & Glue dialog, the Visual Aids group and the Glue switch in the window's own pick of a connector's end are the window's, below).** The window snaps nothing by itself (`crates/ui-egui/src/edit/snapping.rs`, `guides.rs`). **Its drags send their command with `snap: {zoom}`**, `zoom` the view's points to a paper inch (`View::scale`): a move is `shape.move`, a resize `shape.resize`, a drop from the Shapes window (and a click on a tile) `shape.drop`, a connector's free end `connector.add` or `connector.reconnect`. So the engine snaps them by the document's Snap and Glue settings, guides and other shapes included, and holds a box pulled to a guide to it; the answer's `held` names the shapes it holds, and the window keeps the last `snap` for `ui.inspect` (`lastSnap`). A drag ended without `snap` writes the pins, and a held shape is released.
**The preview asks the same question first, through the same functions the commands call, so that it shows where the release lands** (`diacraft_engine::snap::box_of`, `own_points`, `moved_edges`, `is_square` and `snap_resize` (and, `frame_of_shapes`, `dropped_points`, `snap_resize_ext`, `snap_ext` and `snap_turn`) are public, in `snap/subject.rs` and `snap/mod.rs`, and `shape.move`, `shape.resize`, `shape.drop` and `snap.at` call them; no behaviour changed). The window keeps a `SnapPage` (the session's own read of the page, kept for the revision: `Session::snap_page`, which the commands that snap find with `kept_snap_page`) and the document's `SnapSettings` while the drawing, its revision and the page stay as they are (keyed by the drawing's generation as well as the revision, since an opened file's session counts its revisions from 1 again), so that a move of the pointer costs one `snap` query and not a reading of the page and an index of its shapes (36 ms a move on a page of 5,000 shapes when each move read them again). A move works out the box of the shapes that move (`bounds_of`), their connection points (`own_points` with no shift) and, for one shape turned by other than whole quarter turns, its own frame (`frame_of_shapes`) once for the drag, and at each move snaps the box moved by the distance, with the points and the frame moved by it, which is what `shape.move` snaps, to the last bit; Shift is `lock` (the page axis the whole drag has gone farther along, a tie to x, picked again at every move: the free axis snaps and a box pulled to a guide is held); a resize is `snap_resize_ext` on the kept page with `keep` (a corner always, a side with Shift) and `view`, a turned or flipped shape along its own axes, and where no `snap` is sent (Alt, the Snap switch off) the window keeps a corner's proportions itself; the rotation handle is `snap_turn` (another shape's angle within reach, else the step), sent as `shape.rotate` with `snap: {zoom, radius}`; a drop snaps the master's box at the pointer with no shapes moving, since the new shape is not there yet, and with the master's own connection points pulled with it (`dropped_points`, and `dropped_points_of_file` for a master of a stencil file, which reads the places off the shape a drop on a copy of the document makes: the places the new shape will have), as `shape.drop` pulls the new shape's, worked out once for each place of the pointer, and **the drop is the ghost**: a release before the reader has answered, when the ghost went as the pointer made it, is sent without `snap` and lands where the ghost was; an arrow key is `shape.move` with `snap: {nudge: {tick}}` (`tick` one tick of the ruler as the window draws it at the zoom), and Shift with an arrow key a plain move of one screen point (an arrow key asks the reader for the page before it goes, and again after it, so that the next press finds it ready); every snapping command is sent the window's `view` (the visible part of the page); the index of the page's shapes and the targets of a drag are made by a thread of the window's own (`ui-egui` `snap_reader.rs`) on the page the session keeps for the revision, so the first frames of a drag on a big page go as the pointer made them and are sent without `snap`, and the moves after that are queries; **the command that sends the release finds that page**, so that a release, an arrow press, a turn, a resize and a drop build no index of the shapes and no set of targets on the window's thread (a command on a page nobody made ready reads it as before: `diacraft_engine::snap::counts` says how many pages, indexes and sets a thread made; **a drop makes the page ready from the document as it is, before its edit** (it snaps the shape it has just made on the draft of its edit), so that a read the session kept never holds a shape of an edit that was thrown away, and **a new or an opened drawing drops every read the session kept**); a release that changes what it moves by a billionth or more is sent; a connector's free end snaps as `Use::Connect`, as `connector.add` and `connector.reconnect` do, and is drawn glued where it lands on a connection point; the Text tool's drag is a box dragged out from the corner the press landed at, its moved sides snapped as a resize's are, to the width or height of a shape in the view, with a size mark on both boxes (`text.box` takes no `snap`, so the box shown is the box sent); the angle mark of a turn is drawn at the shape's pin (`diacraft_engine::snap::pin_of`), which the shape turns about. A length is sent rounded to a millionth of an inch, and the preview is worked out from the rounded numbers and at the view's zoom (a zoom in the middle of a drag lands it again).
